It's just becoming a real problem for me.\n\nDoctor: When did you first notice that you were having trouble keeping your focus at work?\n\nPatient: Well, it's been going on since my promotion. That was about two years and four months ago. I just found that having a larger portfolio of work started overwhelming the systems I used to rely on to keep myself on track.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble focusing outside of work as well?\n\nPatient: I haven't really noticed it as much at home, to be honest. I mean... I'm not entirely sure, but nothing really stands out to me the way it does at the office. My main concern has just been how much it's impacting my job right now. So, no, not really.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interrupting others or blurting out answers before they finish speaking?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I don't think I do that. I usually just listen... or try to listen, anyway. I'm not really one to jump in or talk over people.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I sit through a lot of long meetings for my job, and staying in my seat isn't an issue for me. I don't really feel a need to get up and move around or fidget with things. I can stay physically still just fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty organizing tasks and activities at work?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I manage a health clinic, so setting up schedules and organizing what needs to get done is a big part of my day. I don't really have any trouble mapping out a plan or figuring out how to arrange things... that part is actually pretty straightforward for me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

I can stay physically still just fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty organizing tasks and activities at work?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I manage a health clinic, so setting up schedules and organizing what needs to get done is a big part of my day. I'm actually on the quieter side, I think. I definitely don't talk too much or dominate conversations. I mean, I guess it wasn't really something people looked for or talked about much when I was younger, so... no, I've never been told I might have it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed difficulty focusing or paying attention when you're doing things you enjoy, like hobbies or leisure activities?\n\nPatient: No, not really. When it's something I actually want to be doing, like reading a good book or gardening, I don't have any trouble focusing. I can just get into it and stay with it for hours. The attention thing only seems to be an issue when it's something I have to do for work or something that feels like a chore.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems in your personal relationships due to your difficulty focusing?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really noticed any problems in my personal relationships because of it. My husband and I get along fine, and my friends haven't mentioned anything. I mean, sometimes I might zone out a bit if a conversation goes on too long about something I'm not that interested in, but it hasn't caused any actual issues or arguments that I'm aware of.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant distress or problems at work or in other areas of your life due to your difficulty with focusing?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has. At work, um, my delays have actually started affecting our clinic reporting deadlines. It's frustrating because I'm making other teams wait on me to get my part done. And in other areas... well, I've had to step back from leading parts of the volunteer health workshops I'm involved with. I just realized I can't reliably keep all the planning pieces moving right now, so I had to let that go.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any other mental health issues, such as depression or anxiety, around the same time as your focus problems started?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't. I mean, obviously I get a bit stressed out when I'm holding up the reporting at the clinic, but... no, no actual anxiety or depression. My mood has been totally fine. It's really just the focus and organization stuff that's been the problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your focus problems are primarily related to the stress or pressure of your job, or do you think they would happen in any situation where you have to concentrate for a long time?\n\nPatient: I'm not entirely sure, to be honest. I mean, my job at the clinic definitely has its pressures, but... I don't think it's just the stress causing it. \n\nLike I mentioned, it happens with my volunteer work too, and that's not really a high-stress environment for me. So, I guess it probably happens in any situation where I have to sit and concentrate on details for a long stretch of time. But I haven't really thought about it enough to say for certain.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

That was about two years and four months ago. I just found that having a larger portfolio of work started overwhelming the systems I used to rely on to keep myself on track.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble focusing outside of work as well?\n\nPatient: I haven't really noticed it as much at home, to be honest. I mean... I'm not entirely sure, but nothing really stands out to me the way it does at the office. My main concern has just been how much it's impacting my job right now. So, no, not really.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interrupting others or blurting out answers before they finish speaking?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I don't think I do that. I usually just listen... or try to listen, anyway. I'm not really one to jump in or talk over people.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I sit through a lot of long meetings for my job, and staying in my seat isn't an issue for me. I don't really feel a need to get up and move around or fidget with things. I can stay physically still just fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty organizing tasks and activities at work?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I manage a health clinic, so setting up schedules and organizing what needs to get done is a big part of my day. I don't really have any trouble mapping out a plan or figuring out how to arrange things... that part is actually pretty straightforward for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself talking excessively?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say so. I'm actually on the quieter side, I think. I definitely don't talk too much or dominate conversations. Like I said, I'm usually just trying to listen.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself avoiding tasks that require a lot of mental effort?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do actually. I find myself putting off the really dense tasks, like policy reviews or long blocks of data analysis, until late in the day. It's just... the thought of sitting down with that much detailed reading and heavy thinking, it feels almost physically hard to start. So I usually end up avoiding it for as long as I can.\n\nDoctor: Do you often make careless mistakes when working on tasks?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think I do. I mean, everyone makes a typo now and then, but I wouldn't say I make careless mistakes on a regular basis. I'm usually pretty careful with my work, especially since I'm dealing with clinic operations. So, no, that hasn't really been an issue for me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with or told you might have attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder, or ADHD?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't. Nobody has ever diagnosed me with it, and no doctor has ever brought it up before. I mean, I guess it wasn't really something people looked for or talked about much when I was younger, so... no, I've never been told I might have it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed difficulty focusing or paying attention when you're doing things you enjoy, like hobbies or leisure activities?\n\nPatient: No, not really. When it's something I actually want to be doing, like reading a good book or gardening, I don't have any trouble focusing. I can just get into it and stay with it for hours. The attention thing only seems to be an issue when it's something I have to do for work or something that feels like a chore.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems in your personal relationships due to your difficulty focusing?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really noticed any problems in my personal relationships because of it. My husband and I get along fine, and my friends haven't mentioned anything. I mean, sometimes I might zone out a bit if a conversation goes on too long about something I'm not that interested in, but it hasn't caused any actual issues or arguments that I'm aware of.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant distress or problems at work or in other areas of your life due to your difficulty with focusing?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has. At work, um, my delays have actually started affecting our clinic reporting deadlines. It's frustrating because I'm making other teams wait on me to get my part done. And in other areas... well, I've had to step back from leading parts of the volunteer health workshops I'm involved with. I just realized I can't reliably keep all the planning pieces moving right now, so I had to let that go.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any other mental health issues, such as depression or anxiety, around the same time as your focus problems started?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't. I mean, obviously I get a bit stressed out when I'm holding up the reporting at the clinic, but... no, no actual anxiety or depression. My mood has been totally fine. It's really just the focus and organization stuff that's been the problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your focus problems are primarily related to the stress or pressure of your job, or do you think they would happen in any situation where you have to concentrate for a long time?\n\nPatient: I'm not entirely sure, to be honest. I mean, my job at the clinic definitely has its pressures, but... I don't think it's just the stress causing it. \n\nLike I mentioned, it happens with my volunteer work too, and that's not really a high-stress environment for me. So, I guess it probably happens in any situation where I have to sit and concentrate on details for a long stretch of time. But I haven't really thought about it enough to say for certain.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty focusing would happen even if you were in a very quiet and comfortable workspace with minimal distractions?\n\nPatient: I think it would still happen, yes. Even when I'm working from home, in my office with the door closed and no noise... I still find my mind wandering off. It's not really about what's going on around me, it's more like I just can't keep my brain locked onto the task, no matter how quiet it is.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{"intent": "determine if the patient's focus problems are persistent across different environments and tasks", "question": "Do you think your difficulty focusing is something that happens every day, or are there some days when you can concentrate just fine?"}

And in other areas... well, I've had to step back from leading parts of the volunteer health workshops I'm involved with. I just realized I can't reliably keep all the planning pieces moving right now, so I had to let that go.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any other mental health issues, such as depression or anxiety, around the same time as your focus problems started?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't. I mean, obviously I get a bit stressed out when I'm holding up the reporting at the clinic, but... no, no actual anxiety or depression. My mood has been totally fine. It's really just the focus and organization stuff that's been the problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your focus problems are primarily related to the stress or pressure of your job, or do you think they would happen in any situation where you have to concentrate for a long time?\n\nPatient: I'm not entirely sure, to be honest. I mean, my job at the clinic definitely has its pressures, but... I don't think it's just the stress causing it. \n\nLike I mentioned, it happens with my volunteer work too, and that's not really a high-stress environment for me. So, I guess it probably happens in any situation where I have to sit and concentrate on details for a long stretch of time. But I haven't really thought about it enough to say for certain.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty focusing would happen even if you were in a very quiet and comfortable workspace with minimal distractions?\n\nPatient: I think it would still happen, yes. Even when I'm working from home, in my office with the door closed and no noise... I still find my mind wandering off. It's not really about what's going on around me, it's more like I just can't keep my brain locked onto the task, no matter how quiet it is.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty focusing is something that happens every day, or are there some days when you can concentrate just fine?\n\nPatient: It's pretty much an everyday thing. I mean, I guess some days are slightly better than others, but I wouldn't say there's ever a day where I can just sit down and concentrate perfectly fine. The struggle to stay focused is always there, every single day, to some degree.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. It's just becoming a real problem for me.\n\nDoctor: When did you first notice that you were having trouble keeping your focus at work?\n\nPatient: Well, it's been going on since my promotion. That was about two years and four months ago. I mean... I'm not entirely sure, but nothing really stands out to me the way it does at the office. My main concern has just been how much it's impacting my job right now. So, no, not really.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interrupting others or blurting out answers before they finish speaking?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I don't think I do that. I can stay physically still just fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty organizing tasks and activities at work?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I manage a health clinic, so setting up schedules and organizing what needs to get done is a big part of my day. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, thank you. Well, what finally pushed me to make this appointment is that I'm having a really hard time keeping my focus at work. \n\nI can sit down to start an EPIC quality report, or go into a clinic metrics meeting with every intention of tracking what's going on. But... within just a few minutes, my mind has drifted so far that I end up rereading the exact same numbers over and over, or I suddenly realize I completely missed the whole point of the discussion. It's just becoming a real problem for me.\n\nDoctor: When did you first notice that you were having trouble keeping your focus at work?\n\nPatient: Well, it's been going on since my promotion. That was about two years and four months ago. I just found that having a larger portfolio of work started overwhelming the systems I used to rely on to keep myself on track.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble focusing outside of work as well?\n\nPatient: I haven't really noticed it as much at home, to be honest. I mean... I'm not entirely sure, but nothing really stands out to me the way it does at the office. My main concern has just been how much it's impacting my job right now. So, no, not really.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interrupting others or blurting out answers before they finish speaking?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I don't think I do that. I usually just listen... or try to listen, anyway. I'm not really one to jump in or talk over people.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I sit through a lot of long meetings for my job, and staying in my seat isn't an issue for me. I don't really feel a need to get up and move around or fidget with things. I can stay physically still just fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty organizing tasks and activities at work?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I manage a health clinic, so setting up schedules and organizing what needs to get done is a big part of my day. I don't really have any trouble mapping out a plan or figuring out how to arrange things... that part is actually pretty straightforward for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself talking excessively?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say so. I'm actually on the quieter side, I think. I definitely don't talk too much or dominate conversations. Like I said, I'm usually just trying to listen.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself avoiding tasks that require a lot of mental effort?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do actually. I find myself putting off the really dense tasks, like policy reviews or long blocks of data analysis, until late in the day. It's just... the thought of sitting down with that much detailed reading and heavy thinking, it feels almost physically hard to start. So I usually end up avoiding it for as long as I can.\n\nDoctor: Do you often make careless mistakes when working on tasks?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think I do. I mean, everyone makes a typo now and then, but I wouldn't say I make careless mistakes on a regular basis. I'm usually pretty careful with my work, especially since I'm dealing with clinic operations. So, no, that hasn't really been an issue for me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with or told you might have attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder, or ADHD?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't. Nobody has ever diagnosed me with it, and no doctor has ever brought it up before. When it's something I actually want to be doing, like reading a good book or gardening, I don't have any trouble focusing. I can just get into it and stay with it for hours. The attention thing only seems to be an issue when it's something I have to do for work or something that feels like a chore.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems in your personal relationships due to your difficulty focusing?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really noticed any problems in my personal relationships because of it. My husband and I get along fine, and my friends haven't mentioned anything.     "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n20\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, thank you. Well, what finally pushed me to make this appointment is that I'm having a really hard time keeping my focus at work. \n\nI can sit down to start an EPIC quality report, or go into a clinic metrics meeting with every intention of tracking what's going on. But... within just a few minutes, my mind has drifted so far that I end up rereading the exact same numbers over and over, or I suddenly realize I completely missed the whole point of the discussion. It's just becoming a real problem for me.\n\nDoctor: When did you first notice that you were having trouble keeping your focus at work?\n\nPatient: Well, it's been going on since my promotion. That was about two years and four months ago. I just found that having a larger portfolio of work started overwhelming the systems I used to rely on to keep myself on track.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble focusing outside of work as well?\n\nPatient: I haven't really noticed it as much at home, to be honest. I mean... I'm not entirely sure, but nothing really stands out to me the way it does at the office. My main concern has just been how much it's impacting my job right now. So, no, not really.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interrupting others or blurting out answers before they finish speaking?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I don't think I do that. I usually just listen... or try to listen, anyway. I'm not really one to jump in or talk over people.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I sit through a lot of long meetings for my job, and staying in my seat isn't an issue for me. I don't really feel a need to get up and move around or fidget with things. I can stay physically still just fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty organizing tasks and activities at work?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I manage a health clinic, so setting up schedules and organizing what needs to get done is a big part of my day. I don't really have any trouble mapping out a plan or figuring out how to arrange things... that part is actually pretty straightforward for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself talking excessively?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say so. I'm actually on the quieter side, I think. I definitely don't talk too much or dominate conversations. Like I said, I'm usually just trying to listen.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself avoiding tasks that require a lot of mental effort?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do actually. I find myself putting off the really dense tasks, like policy reviews or long blocks of data analysis, until late in the day. It's just... the thought of sitting down with that much detailed reading and heavy thinking, it feels almost physically hard to start. So I usually end up avoiding it for as long as I can.\n\nDoctor: Do you often make careless mistakes when working on tasks?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think I do. I mean, everyone makes a typo now and then, but I wouldn't say I make careless mistakes on a regular basis. I'm usually pretty careful with my work, especially since I'm dealing with clinic operations. So, no, that hasn't really been an issue for me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with or told you might have attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder, or ADHD?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't. Nobody has ever diagnosed me with it, and no doctor has ever brought it up before. I mean, I guess it wasn't really something people looked for or talked about much when I was younger, so... no, I've never been told I might have it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed difficulty focusing or paying attention when you're doing things you enjoy, like hobbies or leisure activities?\n\nPatient: No, not really. When it's something I actually want to be doing, like reading a good book or gardening, I don't have any trouble focusing. I can just get into it and stay with it for hours. The attention thing only seems to be an issue when it's something I have to do for work or something that feels like a chore.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems in your personal relationships due to your difficulty focusing?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really noticed any problems in my personal relationships because of it. My husband and I get along fine, and my friends haven't mentioned anything. I mean, sometimes I might zone out a bit if a conversation goes on too long about something I'm not that interested in, but it hasn't caused any actual issues or arguments that I'm aware of.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant distress or problems at work or in other areas of your life due to your difficulty with focusing?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has. At work, um, my delays have actually started affecting our clinic reporting deadlines. It's frustrating because I'm making other teams wait on me to get my part done. And in other areas... well, I've had to step back from leading parts of the volunteer health workshops I'm involved with. I just realized I can't reliably keep all the planning pieces moving right now, so I had to let that go.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any other mental health issues, such as depression or anxiety, around the same time as your focus problems started?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't. I mean, obviously I get a bit stressed out when I'm holding up the reporting at the clinic, but... no, no actual anxiety or depression. My mood has been totally fine. It's really just the focus and organization stuff that's been the problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your focus problems are primarily related to the stress or pressure of your job, or do you think they would happen in any situation where you have to concentrate for a long time?\n\nPatient: I'm not entirely sure, to be honest. I mean, my job at the clinic definitely has its pressures, but... I don't think it's just the stress causing it. \n\nLike I mentioned, it happens with my volunteer work too, and that's not really a high-stress environment for me. So, I guess it probably happens in any situation where I have to sit and concentrate on details for a long stretch of time. But I haven't really thought about it enough to say for certain.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty focusing would happen even if you were in a very quiet and comfortable workspace with minimal distractions?\n\nPatient: I think it would still happen, yes. Even when I'm working from home, in my office with the door closed and no noise... I still find my mind wandering off. It's not really about what's going on around me, it's more like I just can't keep my brain locked onto the task, no matter how quiet it is.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty focusing is something that happens every day, or are there some days when you can concentrate just fine?\n\nPatient: It's pretty much an everyday thing. I mean, I guess some days are slightly better than others, but I wouldn't say there's ever a day where I can just sit down and concentrate perfectly fine. The struggle to stay focused is always there, every single day, to some degree.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your difficulty focusing affecting your performance or relationships outside of work, such as in your volunteer work or personal projects?\n\nPatient: Yes, it definitely affects things outside of work too. Like I mentioned with the volunteer work... I actually had to step back from leading parts of the health workshops we do. I just couldn't reliably keep all the planning pieces moving forward. I was dropping the ball on the details. \n\nAnd at the clinic, it's getting to the point where my delays are affecting our reporting deadlines. Other teams end up waiting on me because I just can't get the data sorted and finished on time. So, yes, it's definitely impacting my performance in both areas.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any problems in your personal relationships, such as with your husband or friends, due to your difficulty with focusing?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, my husband and I are doing fine, and I haven't had any issues with my friends over it. It really just seems to be a problem when I need to get tasks done, like with my job or that volunteer planning. But socially or in my marriage... no, I haven't noticed it causing any problems there.\n\nDoctor: Are your focus problems causing you significant difficulties in both your work and personal life?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was saying, it's really mainly affecting my work and the volunteer stuff. At the clinic, my delays are starting to mess with our reporting deadlines, and it's causing other teams to have to wait on me. And with the volunteer health workshops, I actually had to step back from leading parts of it because I just can't reliably keep all the planning pieces moving forward. But as for my personal life, like my marriage and my friends, no, it hasn't really caused any significant difficulties there.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

But as for my personal life, like my marriage and my friends, no, it hasn't really caused any significant difficulties there."
